88 · Assistant m0088Jev position 18
Relevant 56.0%Not relevant 44.0%
Yes. **Usefulness means improving the answer to the current user message**, not preserving a complete account of how the user arrived there.

The user’s current intention may matter. The evolution of that intention matters only if knowing about the change helps answer the current request.

I’d phrase that part as:

> Preserve messages that clarify the user’s intention only when that understanding helps answer the current user message. Preserve earlier intentions or changes in intention only when knowledge of those changes is useful for answering the current request.

m0088 · assistant · relevance 56.0% · kept · position 18

Yes. **Usefulness means improving the answer to the current user message**, not preserving a complete account of how the user arrived there.

The user’s current intention may matter. The evolution of that intention matters only if knowing about the change helps answer the current request.

I’d phrase that part as:

> Preserve messages that clarify the user’s intention only when that understanding helps answer the current user message. Preserve earlier intentions or changes in intention only when knowledge of those changes is useful for answering the current request.

ModelsJev

# Jev

Restricted

The first System One model. Jev does not generate text: it reads a state plus a typed question and returns a decision with a calibrated probability, in a single parallel pass. Because the output space is defined by the caller, it cannot produce an answer outside it.

Jev explained: TypeSafe AI's decision model | System One Models (https://systemonemodels.org/guides/jev-explained/)
citeturn51search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 days ago; Crawled: 2 days ago; Jev is a model from TypeSafe AI that answers typed questions about text. ... Ask it something and you get back a value with a type, not a sentence. ... Total context is 64k tokens per request.
Treat the direction as credible and the multiple as unverified.

## Limits

Total context is 64k tokens per request. Within that, state plus the longest single question can run to 32k tokens. Rate limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute.

Input is text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values. No image, audio or video input.

The maximum number of questions per request is not documented anywhere in TypeSafe’s docs. Since all questions in a call are evaluated in parallel, the practical ceiling is the 64k context budget.

## Access and SDKs

Jev is a closed managed API in early access, opened off a waitlist. The weights are not released, so there is no self-hosted option.

The endpoint is `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`, authenticated with an `Authorization: Bearer` header. Both SDKs read the key from `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`.
    
    `pip install typesafe-sdk
    npm install @typesafe-ai/sdk`

The Python SDK is `typesafe-sdk` and needs Python 3.10 or newer. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# Jev rate limits and context window

Jev takes 64,000 tokens per request, of which 32,000 is the budget for the state plus the longest question. The account limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute. Input is text only. Input tokens cost $0.042 per 1M, output tokens are free, and there is no free tier.

Every figure on this page, including the summary above, is from TypeSafe's models page and API reference, read on 18 September 2026. The last section works out which limit actually binds you.

## Token budget per request

Limit

Value

Note

Total tokens per request

64,000

State plus every question in the request.

State plus the longest question

32,000

The binding constraint on how much content one call can judge.

Character equivalent of the 32k budget

about 150,000 English characters

TypeSafe's own rule of thumb in the primitives docs.

API

# Jev API reference

One endpoint, one state, as many typed questions as you like — evaluated in parallel and returned in a single response.

## Endpoint

Two hosts, same contract. Use TypeSafe's if you have an early-access key. Use ours if you do not — one is free, and everything documented on this page applies identically to both.

Official endpoint
    `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`

Same shape, our key
    `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. Everything on this page applies to both.

Model routes
    `jev-latest` `jev-preview` `jev-1.13.0`

Auth
    `Authorization: Bearer …` — a TypeSafe key for the first host, a `jv_live_` key for the second. They are not interchangeable, and each host rejects the other's key with a 401.

Context
    64k tokens

Modalities
    Text only — no image or audio input at launch

Latency
    70–500ms end-to-end

SDKs
    Python, JavaScript, plus raw HTTP

## Guides

### What is Jev AI? TypeSafe's decision model explained

Jev is TypeSafe AI's decision model. It answers typed questions about text with calibrated probabilities, and it is not an LLM, because it writes nothing.

Updated 20 Sep 2026

### Jev speed and pricing

Jev costs $0.042 per million input tokens with free output. TypeSafe claims 70ms to 500ms and 40x to 200x. What those numbers cover.

Updated 18 Sep 2026

### How to get Jev API access

How to get Jev API access: the TypeSafe waitlist, the console key page, the TYPESAFE_API_KEY env var, SDK installs, and a first working call.

Updated 18 Sep 2026

### Jev architecture: parameters, encoder or decoder, and no paper yet

TypeSafe has not published Jev's parameter count, base architecture or weights. Here is the public record and what the community has guessed from it.

L22: 
L23: Jev is TypeSafe’s flagship model and the first cite3†System One model . Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.
L24: ##
L25: 
L26: cite5†​ L27: 
L28: Current models
L29: 
L30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`
L31: --- | ---
L32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042
L33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute
L34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question
L35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.
L36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.
L37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .
L38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.
L39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.
L40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].
L41: ##
L42: 
L43: cite11†​ L44: 
L45: Aliases
L46: 
L47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.
L48: 
L49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning
L50: --- | --- | ---
L51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.
L52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.
L53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.
L54: 
L55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.
L56: ##
L57: 
L58: cite12†​ L59: 
L60: Customizing Jev
L61: 
L62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:
L63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .
L64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .
L65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.
L66: ##
L67: 
L68: cite18†​ L69: 
L70: Language support
L71: 
L72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.

43 · Assistant m0043Jev position 9
Relevant 80.0%Not relevant 20.0%
Yes—but there’s a tighter limit that matters for our architecture.

The current official limits for **Jev 1.13**, which `jev-latest` points to, are:

| Limit | What counts |
|---|---|
| **64k tokens per request** | Shared `state` + **all questions combined** |
| **32k tokens** | Shared `state` + **the longest individual question** |

Jev reads the shared state once and evaluates the questions against it in parallel. [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).

**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.

Splitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.

For expanding the benchmark:

- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.
- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.

We can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt.

User message

We could chunk the history into 32k blocks to run against jev?

Without Jev

Yes. Chunking into ~32k-token blocks is a practical way to extend Jev-based curation beyond its per-request state limit.

The main caveat: **independent chunk decisions are not globally informed**. A message in an early chunk may only be important because of the final user request or because of information introduced in a later chunk. So every chunk should be judged against the same compact representation of the target task and any known global facts.

A robust design would be a **hierarchical / map-reduce curation flow**:

1. **Reserve headroom**
   - Keep each Jev `state` well under 32k tokens, e.g. **26–28k history tokens**.
   - Reserve room for the longest curation question, message IDs, roles, and formatting.
   - Also batch questions so each request remains below the **64k total** limit.

2. **Chunk by semantic boundaries**
   - Never split inside a message.
   - For tool use, keep a tool call and its corresponding result in the same chunk.
   - Prefer cuts at turn boundaries; include a small overlap at cuts when cross-boundary references are likely.

3. **Give every chunk the final target**
   - State for a chunk includes:
     - compact current task / latest user request,
     - the chunk’s messages,
     - a clear statement that omitted context may exist before and after,
     - the existing retain/omit criteria.
   - Jev marks messages worth retaining from that chunk.

4. **Always retain structural anchors**
   - System/developer instructions.
   - The current user request.
   - Tool-call/result pairs required for causal validity.
   - Optionally the first message of a conversation or explicit user preferences.

5. **Second pass over survivors**
   - Combine all first-pass retained messages, in original chronological order.
   - If this survivor set fits within ~28k tokens, run the current high-quality prompt once more as a global refinement pass.
   - This removes false positives accumulated independently across chunks and restores global judgment.

6. **If survivors still exceed capacity**
   - Repeat the map/reduce layer: chunk the survivors, curate each chunk, then refine again.
   - Stop when the retained set fits the final-pass budget, or use a configured maximum number of layers.
   - Record each layer’s selections and reason codes so the benchmark can audit why a message remained.

The key potential failure is **false-negative loss in the first pass**: if an early message is dropped, later passes cannot recover it. To reduce that risk, make the first pass deliberately recall-oriented:

- use a **lower retain threshold** in chunk passes;
- instruct Jev to retain ambiguous messages that could establish constraints, facts, commitments, entities, unresolved questions, or dependencies;
- use a stricter threshold only in the final global pass.

For tool traces, I would treat each tool-call/result sequence as an **atomic group** during selection. If any member is retained, retain the complete linked group; otherwise the downstream model may see an orphaned result or a call without its output.

So: chunking works, but it should be a **recall-first hierarchical process**, not simply “run the existing omission prompt separately on each 32k slice and concatenate the outputs.”

With Jev

Yes. We can chunk the history into blocks that each fit Jev’s **32k `state` + longest-question** constraint.

But it should be treated as a **hierarchical curation pass**, rather than assuming independent 32k chunks reproduce a full-history decision.

### Practical approach

1. **Split the conversation into ~24–28k-token blocks**
   - Reserve the remaining room for the per-message preservation question, metadata, and safety margin.
   - Prefer semantic boundaries: turns, tool-call/result groups, or task phases—not arbitrary token boundaries.

2. **Run Jev within each block**
   - Ask which messages must survive *within that local context*.
   - Keep messages needed for local coherence, facts, decisions, constraints, and unresolved work.

3. **Carry forward a compact cross-block “ledger”**
   - Before evaluating a later block, prepend a small structured state containing surviving facts/decisions from earlier blocks:
     - user preferences and constraints
     - decisions made
     - task status
     - unresolved questions
     - identifiers/references needed later
   - This lets later chunks recognize dependencies without shipping all earlier raw messages.

4. **Merge retained messages and optionally run a final pass**
   - The union of retained messages will usually be much smaller than the original history.
   - If that merged set fits Jev’s window, run one final global curation pass.
   - If it still does not fit, recurse: chunk the retained set again, while preserving the ledger.

### Important caveat

Naively scoring each 32k block independently will miss long-range dependencies. For example, a user might state a requirement in block 1 that makes an apparently unimportant decision in block 4 essential. The cross-block ledger—and ideally a final reconciliation pass—is what makes chunking viable.

### For tool usage

Tool interactions should be chunked as **atomic groups**:

```text
assistant tool call
tool result
assistant interpretation / action
```

We should avoid retaining a tool result without its call, or retaining a call without its result, unless we deliberately replace the whole group with a structured summary. For large outputs, the ledger can retain the important extracted facts rather than the full raw result.

So: **yes, chunking removes the hard 32k-history ceiling**, but turns the design into a map/reduce or recursive compaction system. The large-context scenario should specifically test whether it preserves dependencies across chunk boundaries.

Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.\nL24: ##\nL25: \nL26: cite5†​ L27: \nL28: Current models\nL29: \nL30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`\nL31: --- | ---\nL32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042\nL33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute\nL34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question\nL35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.\nL36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.\nL37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .\nL38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.\nL39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.\nL40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].\nL41: ##\nL42: \nL43: cite11†​ L44: \nL45: Aliases\nL46: \nL47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.\nL48: \nL49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning\nL50: --- | --- | ---\nL51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.\nL52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.\nL53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.\nL54: \nL55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.\nL56: ##\nL57: \nL58: cite12†​ L59: \nL60: Customizing Jev\nL61: \nL62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:\nL63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .\nL64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .\nL65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.\nL66: ##\nL67: \nL68: cite18†​ L69: \nL70: Language support\nL71: \nL72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.\nL73: ##\nL74: \nL75: cite20†​ L76: \nL77: Data handling\nL78: \nL79: Jev is not trained on customer requests or responses. See cite21†Legal for the Data Processing Agreement, the Privacy Policy, and details on zero data retention (ZDR) for enterprise customers.\nL80: ##\nL81: \nL82: cite22†​ L83: \nL84: Listing models\nL85: \nL86: `GET /v1/models` returns the names your account can send in the `model` field, with a description and release date for each. It currently lists the aliases. Versioned IDs such as `jev-1.13.0` are accepted by the `model` field whether or not they appear in the list.\nL87: \nL88: cURL\nL89: \nL90: Python\nL91: JavaScript\nL92: \nL93:     `curl [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).\n\n**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.\n\nSplitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.\n\nFor expanding the benchmark:\n\n- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.\n- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.\n\nWe can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt."

ModelsJev

# Jev

Restricted

The first System One model. Jev does not generate text: it reads a state plus a typed question and returns a decision with a calibrated probability, in a single parallel pass. Because the output space is defined by the caller, it cannot produce an answer outside it.

Jev explained: TypeSafe AI's decision model | System One Models (https://systemonemodels.org/guides/jev-explained/)
citeturn51search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 days ago; Crawled: 2 days ago; Jev is a model from TypeSafe AI that answers typed questions about text. ... Ask it something and you get back a value with a type, not a sentence. ... Total context is 64k tokens per request.
Treat the direction as credible and the multiple as unverified.

## Limits

Total context is 64k tokens per request. Within that, state plus the longest single question can run to 32k tokens. Rate limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute.

Input is text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values. No image, audio or video input.

The maximum number of questions per request is not documented anywhere in TypeSafe’s docs. Since all questions in a call are evaluated in parallel, the practical ceiling is the 64k context budget.

## Access and SDKs

Jev is a closed managed API in early access, opened off a waitlist. The weights are not released, so there is no self-hosted option.

The endpoint is `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`, authenticated with an `Authorization: Bearer` header. Both SDKs read the key from `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`.
    
    `pip install typesafe-sdk
    npm install @typesafe-ai/sdk`

The Python SDK is `typesafe-sdk` and needs Python 3.10 or newer. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------

# Jev rate limits and context window

Jev takes 64,000 tokens per request, of which 32,000 is the budget for the state plus the longest question. The account limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute. Input is text only. Input tokens cost $0.042 per 1M, output tokens are free, and there is no free tier.

Every figure on this page, including the summary above, is from TypeSafe's models page and API reference, read on 18 September 2026. The last section works out which limit actually binds you.

## Token budget per request

Limit

Value

Note

Total tokens per request

64,000

State plus every question in the request.

State plus the longest question

32,000

The binding constraint on how much content one call can judge.

Character equivalent of the 32k budget

about 150,000 English characters

TypeSafe's own rule of thumb in the primitives docs.

API

# Jev API reference

One endpoint, one state, as many typed questions as you like — evaluated in parallel and returned in a single response.

## Endpoint

Two hosts, same contract. Use TypeSafe's if you have an early-access key. Use ours if you do not — one is free, and everything documented on this page applies identically to both.

Official endpoint
    `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`

Same shape, our key
    `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. Everything on this page applies to both.

Model routes
    `jev-latest` `jev-preview` `jev-1.13.0`

Auth
    `Authorization: Bearer …` — a TypeSafe key for the first host, a `jv_live_` key for the second. They are not interchangeable, and each host rejects the other's key with a 401.

Context
    64k tokens

Modalities
    Text only — no image or audio input at launch

Latency
    70–500ms end-to-end

SDKs
    Python, JavaScript, plus raw HTTP

## Guides

### What is Jev AI? TypeSafe's decision model explained

Jev is TypeSafe AI's decision model. It answers typed questions about text with calibrated probabilities, and it is not an LLM, because it writes nothing.

Updated 20 Sep 2026

### Jev speed and pricing

Jev costs $0.042 per million input tokens with free output. TypeSafe claims 70ms to 500ms and 40x to 200x. What those numbers cover.

Updated 18 Sep 2026

### How to get Jev API access

How to get Jev API access: the TypeSafe waitlist, the console key page, the TYPESAFE_API_KEY env var, SDK installs, and a first working call.

Updated 18 Sep 2026

### Jev architecture: parameters, encoder or decoder, and no paper yet

TypeSafe has not published Jev's parameter count, base architecture or weights. Here is the public record and what the community has guessed from it.

Accuracy falls as the state grows with detail the question does not need, and `jev-1.13` takes 64k tokens for the state and questions together, with 32k for the state plus the longest question; past that the request fails with a `ModelHTTPError` (`max_tokens_exceeded`), which a `FallbackModel` hands to the model behind Jev like any API error, so an over-long conversation quietly becomes a language model call. ... A function tool with arguments is not called by Jev either, but proposed for a model behind it; with tools attached, the output type needs a docstring or the agent instructions to be weighed against them.  * No image, audio, video or document in the prompt or the history.

L19: # Models
L20: 
L21: Copy page Copy page
L22: 
L23: Jev is TypeSafe’s flagship model and the first cite3†System One model . Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.
L24: ##
L25: 
L26: cite5†​ L27: 
L28: Current models
L29: 
L30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`
L31: --- | ---
L32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042
L33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute
L34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question
L35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.
L36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.
L37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .
L38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.
L39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.
L40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].
L41: ##
L42: 
L43: cite11†​ L44: 
L45: Aliases
L46: 
L47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.
L48: 
L49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning
L50: --- | --- | ---
L51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.
L52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.
L53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.
L54: 
L55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.
L56: ##
L57: 
L58: cite12†​ L59: 
L60: Customizing Jev
L61: 
L62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:
L63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .
L64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .
L65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.
L66: ##
L67: 
L68: cite18†​ L69: 
L70: Language support
L71: 
L72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.
L73: ##
L74: 
L75: cite20†​ L76: 
L77: Data handling
L78: 
L79: Jev is not trained on customer requests or responses. See cite21†Legal for the Data Processing Agreement, the Privacy Policy, and details on zero data retention (ZDR) for enterprise customers.
L80: ##

m0043 · assistant · relevance 80.0% · kept · position 9

Yes—but there’s a tighter limit that matters for our architecture.

The current official limits for **Jev 1.13**, which `jev-latest` points to, are:

| Limit | What counts |
|---|---|
| **64k tokens per request** | Shared `state` + **all questions combined** |
| **32k tokens** | Shared `state` + **the longest individual question** |

Jev reads the shared state once and evaluates the questions against it in parallel. [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).

**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.

Splitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.

For expanding the benchmark:

- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.
- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.

We can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt.

Ask it something and you get back a value with a type, not a sentence. ... Total context is 64k tokens per request.\nTreat the direction as credible and the multiple as unverified.\n\n## Limits\n\nTotal context is 64k tokens per request. Within that, state plus the longest single question can run to 32k tokens. Rate limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute.\n\nInput is text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values. No image, audio or video input.\n\nThe maximum number of questions per request is not documented anywhere in TypeSafe’s docs. Since all questions in a call are evaluated in parallel, the practical ceiling is the 64k context budget.\n\n## Access and SDKs\n\nJev is a closed managed API in early access, opened off a waitlist. The weights are not released, so there is no self-hosted option.\n\nThe endpoint is `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`, authenticated with an `Authorization: Bearer` header. Both SDKs read the key from `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`.\n    \n    `pip install typesafe-sdk\n    npm install @typesafe-ai/sdk`\n\nThe Python SDK is `typesafe-sdk` and needs Python 3.10 or newer. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev rate limits, context window and pricing | OpenTweet (https://opentweet.io/jev/limits)\nciteturn51search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; # Jev rate limits and context windowJev takes 64,000 tokens per request, of which 32,000 is the budget for the state plus the longest question. ... @typesafe-ai/sdk, Node 20 or newer ... 'Content-Type': 'application/json',\n\nHomeJevLimits\n\nLast updated: September 2026\n\n# Jev rate limits and context window\n\nJev takes 64,000 tokens per request, of which 32,000 is the budget for the state plus the longest question. Input tokens cost $0.042 per 1M, output tokens are free, and there is no free tier.\n\nEvery figure on this page, including the summary above, is from TypeSafe's models page and API reference, read on 18 September 2026. Use TypeSafe's if you have an early-access key. Use ours if you do not — one is free, and everything documented on this page applies identically to both.\n\nOfficial endpoint\n    `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`\n\nSame shape, our key\n    `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. Everything on this page applies to both.\n\nModel routes\n    `jev-latest` `jev-preview` `jev-1.13.0`\n\nAuth\n    `Authorization: Bearer …` — a TypeSafe key for the first host, a `jv_live_` key for the second. They are not interchangeable, and each host rejects the other's key with a 401.\n\nContext\n    64k tokens\n\nModalities\n    Text only — no image or audio input at launch\n\nLatency\n    70–500ms end-to-end\n\nSDKs\n    Python, JavaScript, plus raw HTTP\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev 1.13 by TypeSafe AI: specs, limits, pricing and versions | System One Models (https://systemonemodels.org/models/jev/)\nciteturn51search4 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 days ago; Crawled: today; Total context  | 64,000 tokens ... 64k tokens across the whole request. ... Jev is TypeSafe AI's decision model.\n## Guides\n\n### What is Jev AI? TypeSafe's decision model explained\n\nJev is TypeSafe AI's decision model. It answers typed questions about text with calibrated probabilities, and it is not an LLM, because it writes nothing.\n\nUpdated 20 Sep 2026\n\n### Jev speed and pricing\n\nJev costs $0.042 per million input tokens with free output. TypeSafe claims 70ms to 500ms and 40x to 200x. What those numbers cover.\n\nUpdated 18 Sep 2026\n\n### How to get Jev API access\n\nHow to get Jev API access: the TypeSafe waitlist, the console key page, the TYPESAFE_API_KEY env var, SDK installs, and a first working call.\n\nUpdated 18 Sep 2026\n\n### Jev architecture: parameters, encoder or decoder, and no paper yet\n\nTypeSafe has not published Jev's parameter count, base architecture or weights. Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.\nL24: ##\nL25: \nL26: cite5†​ L27: \nL28: Current models\nL29: \nL30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`\nL31: --- | ---\nL32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042\nL33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute\nL34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question\nL35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.\nL36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.\nL37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .\nL38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.\nL39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.\nL40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].\nL41: ##\nL42: \nL43: cite11†​ L44: \nL45: Aliases\nL46: \nL47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.\nL48: \nL49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning\nL50: --- | --- | ---\nL51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.\nL52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.\nL53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.\nL54: \nL55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.\nL56: ##\nL57: \nL58: cite12†​ L59: \nL60: Customizing Jev\nL61: \nL62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:\nL63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .\nL64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .\nL65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.\nL66: ##\nL67: \nL68: cite18†​ L69: \nL70: Language support\nL71: \nL72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.\nL73: ##\nL74: \nL75: cite20†​ L76: \nL77: Data handling\nL78: \nL79: Jev is not trained on customer requests or responses. See cite21†Legal for the Data Processing Agreement, the Privacy Policy, and details on zero data retention (ZDR) for enterprise customers.\nL80: ##\nL81: \nL82: cite22†​ L83: \nL84: Listing models\nL85: \nL86: `GET /v1/models` returns the names your account can send in the `model` field, with a description and release date for each. It currently lists the aliases. [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).\n\n**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.\n\nSplitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.\n\nFor expanding the benchmark:\n\n- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.\n- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.\n\nWe can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt."

                "content": "Yes. Chunking into ~32k-token blocks is a practical way to extend Jev-based curation beyond its per-request state limit.\n\nThe main caveat: **independent chunk decisions are not globally informed**. A message in an early chunk may only be important because of the final user request or because of information introduced in a later chunk. So every chunk should be judged against the same compact representation of the target task and any known global facts.\n\nA robust design would be a **hierarchical / map-reduce curation flow**:\n\n1. **Reserve headroom**\n   - Keep each Jev `state` well under 32k tokens, e.g. **26–28k history tokens**.\n   - Reserve room for the longest curation question, message IDs, roles, and formatting.\n   - Also batch questions so each request remains below the **64k total** limit.\n\n2. **Chunk by semantic boundaries**\n   - Never split inside a message.\n   - For tool use, keep a tool call and its corresponding result in the same chunk.\n   - Prefer cuts at turn boundaries; include a small overlap at cuts when cross-boundary references are likely.\n\n3. **Give every chunk the final target**\n   - State for a chunk includes:\n     - compact current task / latest user request,\n     - the chunk’s messages,\n     - a clear statement that omitted context may exist before and after,\n     - the existing retain/omit criteria.\n   - Jev marks messages worth retaining from that chunk.\n\n4. **Always retain structural anchors**\n   - System/developer instructions.\n   - The current user request.\n   - Tool-call/result pairs required for causal validity.\n   - Optionally the first message of a conversation or explicit user preferences.\n\n5. **Second pass over survivors**\n   - Combine all first-pass retained messages, in original chronological order.\n   - If this survivor set fits within ~28k tokens, run the current high-quality prompt once more as a global refinement pass.\n   - This removes false positives accumulated independently across chunks and restores global judgment.\n\n6. **If survivors still exceed capacity**\n   - Repeat the map/reduce layer: chunk the survivors, curate each chunk, then refine again.\n   - Stop when the retained set fits the final-pass budget, or use a configured maximum number of layers.\n   - Record each layer’s selections and reason codes so the benchmark can audit why a message remained.\n\nThe key potential failure is **false-negative loss in the first pass**: if an early message is dropped, later passes cannot recover it. To reduce that risk, make the first pass deliberately recall-oriented:\n\n- use a **lower retain threshold** in chunk passes;\n- instruct Jev to retain ambiguous messages that could establish constraints, facts, commitments, entities, unresolved questions, or dependencies;\n- use a stricter threshold only in the final global pass.\n\nFor tool traces, I would treat each tool-call/result sequence as an **atomic group** during selection. If any member is retained, retain the complete linked group; otherwise the downstream model may see an orphaned result or a call without its output.\n\nSo: chunking works, but it should be a **recall-first hierarchical process**, not simply “run the existing omission prompt separately on each 32k slice and concatenate the outputs.”",

      "text": "Yes. Chunking into ~32k-token blocks is a practical way to extend Jev-based curation beyond its per-request state limit.\n\nThe main caveat: **independent chunk decisions are not globally informed**. A message in an early chunk may only be important because of the final user request or because of information introduced in a later chunk. So every chunk should be judged against the same compact representation of the target task and any known global facts.\n\nA robust design would be a **hierarchical / map-reduce curation flow**:\n\n1. **Reserve headroom**\n   - Keep each Jev `state` well under 32k tokens, e.g. **26–28k history tokens**.\n   - Reserve room for the longest curation question, message IDs, roles, and formatting.\n   - Also batch questions so each request remains below the **64k total** limit.\n\n2. **Chunk by semantic boundaries**\n   - Never split inside a message.\n   - For tool use, keep a tool call and its corresponding result in the same chunk.\n   - Prefer cuts at turn boundaries; include a small overlap at cuts when cross-boundary references are likely.\n\n3. **Give every chunk the final target**\n   - State for a chunk includes:\n     - compact current task / latest user request,\n     - the chunk’s messages,\n     - a clear statement that omitted context may exist before and after,\n     - the existing retain/omit criteria.\n   - Jev marks messages worth retaining from that chunk.\n\n4. **Always retain structural anchors**\n   - System/developer instructions.\n   - The current user request.\n   - Tool-call/result pairs required for causal validity.\n   - Optionally the first message of a conversation or explicit user preferences.\n\n5. **Second pass over survivors**\n   - Combine all first-pass retained messages, in original chronological order.\n   - If this survivor set fits within ~28k tokens, run the current high-quality prompt once more as a global refinement pass.\n   - This removes false positives accumulated independently across chunks and restores global judgment.\n\n6. **If survivors still exceed capacity**\n   - Repeat the map/reduce layer: chunk the survivors, curate each chunk, then refine again.\n   - Stop when the retained set fits the final-pass budget, or use a configured maximum number of layers.\n   - Record each layer’s selections and reason codes so the benchmark can audit why a message remained.\n\nThe key potential failure is **false-negative loss in the first pass**: if an early message is dropped, later passes cannot recover it. To reduce that risk, make the first pass deliberately recall-oriented:\n\n- use a **lower retain threshold** in chunk passes;\n- instruct Jev to retain ambiguous messages that could establish constraints, facts, commitments, entities, unresolved questions, or dependencies;\n- use a stricter threshold only in the final global pass.\n\nFor tool traces, I would treat each tool-call/result sequence as an **atomic group** during selection. If any member is retained, retain the complete linked group; otherwise the downstream model may see an orphaned result or a call without its output.\n\nSo: chunking works, but it should be a **recall-first hierarchical process**, not simply “run the existing omission prompt separately on each 32k slice and concatenate the outputs.”",

    "text": "Yes. Chunking into ~32k-token blocks is a practical way to extend Jev-based curation beyond its per-request state limit.\n\nThe main caveat: **independent chunk decisions are not globally informed**. A message in an early chunk may only be important because of the final user request or because of information introduced in a later chunk. So every chunk should be judged against the same compact representation of the target task and any known global facts.\n\nA robust design would be a **hierarchical / map-reduce curation flow**:\n\n1. **Reserve headroom**\n   - Keep each Jev `state` well under 32k tokens, e.g. **26–28k history tokens**.\n   - Reserve room for the longest curation question, message IDs, roles, and formatting.\n   - Also batch questions so each request remains below the **64k total** limit.\n\n2. **Chunk by semantic boundaries**\n   - Never split inside a message.\n   - For tool use, keep a tool call and its corresponding result in the same chunk.\n   - Prefer cuts at turn boundaries; include a small overlap at cuts when cross-boundary references are likely.\n\n3. **Give every chunk the final target**\n   - State for a chunk includes:\n     - compact current task / latest user request,\n     - the chunk’s messages,\n     - a clear statement that omitted context may exist before and after,\n     - the existing retain/omit criteria.\n   - Jev marks messages worth retaining from that chunk.\n\n4. **Always retain structural anchors**\n   - System/developer instructions.\n   - The current user request.\n   - Tool-call/result pairs required for causal validity.\n   - Optionally the first message of a conversation or explicit user preferences.\n\n5. **Second pass over survivors**\n   - Combine all first-pass retained messages, in original chronological order.\n   - If this survivor set fits within ~28k tokens, run the current high-quality prompt once more as a global refinement pass.\n   - This removes false positives accumulated independently across chunks and restores global judgment.\n\n6. **If survivors still exceed capacity**\n   - Repeat the map/reduce layer: chunk the survivors, curate each chunk, then refine again.\n   - Stop when the retained set fits the final-pass budget, or use a configured maximum number of layers.\n   - Record each layer’s selections and reason codes so the benchmark can audit why a message remained.\n\nThe key potential failure is **false-negative loss in the first pass**: if an early message is dropped, later passes cannot recover it. To reduce that risk, make the first pass deliberately recall-oriented:\n\n- use a **lower retain threshold** in chunk passes;\n- instruct Jev to retain ambiguous messages that could establish constraints, facts, commitments, entities, unresolved questions, or dependencies;\n- use a stricter threshold only in the final global pass.\n\nFor tool traces, I would treat each tool-call/result sequence as an **atomic group** during selection. If any member is retained, retain the complete linked group; otherwise the downstream model may see an orphaned result or a call without its output.\n\nSo: chunking works, but it should be a **recall-first hierarchical process**, not simply “run the existing omission prompt separately on each 32k slice and concatenate the outputs.”",

Total context is 64k tokens per request.\nTreat the direction as credible and the multiple as unverified.\n\n## Limits\n\nTotal context is 64k tokens per request. Within that, state plus the longest single question can run to 32k tokens. Rate limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute.\n\nInput is text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values. No image, audio or video input.\n\nThe maximum number of questions per request is not documented anywhere in TypeSafe’s docs. Since all questions in a call are evaluated in parallel, the practical ceiling is the 64k context budget.\n\n## Access and SDKs\n\nJev is a closed managed API in early access, opened off a waitlist. The weights are not released, so there is no self-hosted option.\n\nThe endpoint is `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`, authenticated with an `Authorization: Bearer` header. Both SDKs read the key from `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`.\n    \n    `pip install typesafe-sdk\n    npm install @typesafe-ai/sdk`\n\nThe Python SDK is `typesafe-sdk` and needs Python 3.10 or newer. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev rate limits, context window and pricing | OpenTweet (https://opentweet.io/jev/limits)\nciteturn51search2 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: yesterday; # Jev rate limits and context windowJev takes 64,000 tokens per request, of which 32,000 is the budget for the state plus the longest question. ... @typesafe-ai/sdk, Node 20 or newer ... The account limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute. Input is text only. Input tokens cost $0.042 per 1M, output tokens are free, and there is no free tier.\n\nEvery figure on this page, including the summary above, is from TypeSafe's models page and API reference, read on 18 September 2026. The last section works out which limit actually binds you.\n\n## Token budget per request\n\nLimit\n\nValue\n\nNote\n\nTotal tokens per request\n\n64,000\n\nState plus every question in the request.\n\nState plus the longest question\n\n32,000\n\nThe binding constraint on how much content one call can judge.\n\nCharacter equivalent of the 32k budget\n\nabout 150,000 English characters\n\nTypeSafe's own rule of thumb in the primitives docs.\n\nQuestions per request\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev API reference — /v1/systemone, Choice, Score & Noul | Jev Agent (https://jev-agent.com/api-reference)\nciteturn51search3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today;     `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone` ...     `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. ... Use TypeSafe's if you have an early-access key. Use ours if you do not — one is free, and everything documented on this page applies identically to both.\n\nOfficial endpoint\n    `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`\n\nSame shape, our key\n    `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. Everything on this page applies to both.\n\nModel routes\n    `jev-latest` `jev-preview` `jev-1.13.0`\n\nAuth\n    `Authorization: Bearer …` — a TypeSafe key for the first host, a `jv_live_` key for the second. They are not interchangeable, and each host rejects the other's key with a 401.\n\nContext\n    64k tokens\n\nModalities\n    Text only — no image or audio input at launch\n\nLatency\n    70–500ms end-to-end\n\nSDKs\n    Python, JavaScript, plus raw HTTP\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev 1.13 by TypeSafe AI: specs, limits, pricing and versions | System One Models (https://systemonemodels.org/models/jev/)\nciteturn51search4 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 days ago; Crawled: today; Total context  | 64,000 tokens ... 64k tokens across the whole request. ... Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.\nL24: ##\nL25: \nL26: cite5†​ L27: \nL28: Current models\nL29: \nL30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`\nL31: --- | ---\nL32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042\nL33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute\nL34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question\nL35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.\nL36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.\nL37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .\nL38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.\nL39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.\nL40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].\nL41: ##\nL42: \nL43: cite11†​ L44: \nL45: Aliases\nL46: \nL47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.\nL48: \nL49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning\nL50: --- | --- | ---\nL51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.\nL52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.\nL53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.\nL54: \nL55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.\nL56: ##\nL57: \nL58: cite12†​ L59: \nL60: Customizing Jev\nL61: \nL62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:\nL63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .\nL64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .\nL65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.\nL66: ##\nL67: \nL68: cite18†​ L69: \nL70: Language support\nL71: \nL72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.\nL73: ##\nL74: \nL75: cite20†​ L76: \nL77: Data handling\nL78: \nL79: Jev is not trained on customer requests or responses. See cite21†Legal for the Data Processing Agreement, the Privacy Policy, and details on zero data retention (ZDR) for enterprise customers.\nL80: ##\nL81: \nL82: cite22†​ L83: \nL84: Listing models\nL85: \nL86: `GET /v1/models` returns the names your account can send in the `model` field, with a description and release date for each. It currently lists the aliases. Versioned IDs such as `jev-1.13.0` are accepted by the `model` field whether or not they appear in the list.\nL87: \nL88: cURL\nL89: \nL90: Python\nL91: JavaScript\nL92: \nL93:     `curl https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/models \\\nL94:       -H \"Authorization: Bearer $TYPESAFE_API_KEY\"\nL95:     `\nL96: \nL97:     `from typesafe_sdk import TypeSafeClient\nL98: \nL99:     with TypeSafeClient() as client:\nL100:         for model in client.models.list().models:\nL101:             print(model.name, model.release_date, model.description)\nL102:     `\nL103: \nL104:     `import { TypeSafeClient } from \"@typesafe-ai/sdk\";\nL105: \nL106:     const client = new TypeSafeClient();\nL107:     const models = await client.models.list();\nL108:     for (const model of models) {\nL109:       console.log(model.name, model.release_date, model.description);\nL110:     }\nL111:     `\nL112: cite23†​ L113: \nL114: [Button: models]\nL115: \nL116: array\nL117: \nL118: required\nL119: \nL120: One entry per model or alias.\nL121: \nL122: Show properties\nL123: \nL124: cite24†​ L125: \nL126: [Button: name]\nL127: \nL128: string\nL129: \nL130: required\nL131: \nL132: The model ID or alias, as accepted by the `model` field.\nL133: \nL134: cite25†​ L135: \nL136: [Button: description]\nL137: \nL138: string\nL139: \nL140: required\nL141: \nL142: What the model is for.\nL143: \nL144: cite26†​ L145: \nL146: [Button: release_date]\nL147: \nL148: string\nL149: \nL150: required\nL151: \nL152: When the model or alias was released.\nL153: \nL154: See the cite27†Python and cite28†JavaScript SDK references for the full method signatures.\nL155: \nL156: Was this page helpful?\nL157: \nL158: Yes No\nL159: cite29†Function: score()Previous [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).\n\n**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.\n\nSplitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.\n\nFor expanding the benchmark:\n\n- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.\n- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.\n\nWe can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt."

Within that, state plus the longest single question can run to 32k tokens. Rate limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute.\n\nInput is text only: a string, a JSON object, or an array of text values. Since all questions in a call are evaluated in parallel, the practical ceiling is the 64k context budget.\n\n## Access and SDKs\n\nJev is a closed managed API in early access, opened off a waitlist. The account limits are 250,000 tokens per second and 1,200 requests per minute. Input is text only. Input tokens cost $0.042 per 1M, output tokens are free, and there is no free tier.\n\nEvery figure on this page, including the summary above, is from TypeSafe's models page and API reference, read on 18 September 2026. The last section works out which limit actually binds you.\n\n## Token budget per request\n\nLimit\n\nValue\n\nNote\n\nTotal tokens per request\n\n64,000\n\nState plus every question in the request.\n\nState plus the longest question\n\n32,000\n\nThe binding constraint on how much content one call can judge.\n\nCharacter equivalent of the 32k budget\n\nabout 150,000 English characters\n\nTypeSafe's own rule of thumb in the primitives docs.\n\nQuestions per request\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev API reference — /v1/systemone, Choice, Score & Noul | Jev Agent (https://jev-agent.com/api-reference)\nciteturn51search3 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today;     `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone` ...     `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. ... Use TypeSafe's if you have an early-access key. Use ours if you do not — one is free, and everything documented on this page applies identically to both.\n\nOfficial endpoint\n    `POST https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone`\n\nSame shape, our key\n    `POST https://jev-agent.com/api/v1/systemone`Field-for-field identical, for a free key from /api-access if you are still on the waitlist. Everything on this page applies to both.\n\nModel routes\n    `jev-latest` `jev-preview` `jev-1.13.0`\n\nAuth\n    `Authorization: Bearer …` — a TypeSafe key for the first host, a `jv_live_` key for the second. They are not interchangeable, and each host rejects the other's key with a 401.\n\nContext\n    64k tokens\n\nModalities\n    Text only — no image or audio input at launch\n\nLatency\n    70–500ms end-to-end\n\nSDKs\n    Python, JavaScript, plus raw HTTP\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nJev 1.13 by TypeSafe AI: specs, limits, pricing and versions | System One Models (https://systemonemodels.org/models/jev/)\nciteturn51search4 [wordlim: 200] Published: 6 days ago; Crawled: today; Total context  | 64,000 tokens ... 64k tokens across the whole request. ... Every model on this page is served by the same endpoint, `POST /v1/systemone`. The request’s `model` field selects which one handles the call; see the cite4†API reference for the full request shape.\nL24: ##\nL25: \nL26: cite5†​ L27: \nL28: Current models\nL29: \nL30: Jev 1.13  | `jev-1.13.0`\nL31: --- | ---\nL32: Price (per Btok / per Mtok)  | $42 / $0.042\nL33: Rate limits  | 250,000 tokens per second / 1,200 requests per minute\nL34: Context length  | 64k tokens per request; 32k tokens for `state` plus the longest question\nL35: Input  | Text only. String, JSON object, or array of text values. No image, audio, or video input.\nL36:   * Price: Charged per input token. Output tokens are free. A Btok is a billion tokens and an Mtok is a million tokens.\nL37:   * Rate limits: Measured in tokens per second and requests per minute. A request over either limit returns `429 Too Many Requests`. Our cite6†client SDKs retry with backoff by default and honor the `retry-after` header when the response carries one. If you call the HTTP API directly, see cite7†Handling rate limits .\nL38:   * Context length: Jev ingests the `state` once and evaluates every question against it in parallel. The 64k budget covers the `state` plus all questions combined; the 32k budget applies to the `state` plus the single longest question. See cite8†Speculative fan-out for packing many questions into one request, and cite9†Jev 1.13 jaggedness for how accuracy shifts as the state grows.\nL39:   * Input: Jev evaluates natural-language text. Pre-process non-text inputs (images, audio, video, binaries) into text or structured fields before sending them as `state`. See cite10†State for supported shapes.\nL40: Rate limits are adjusting dynamically. We are serving a very large volume of demand, and the limits above can change without notice while we do, as upcoming large GPU deals land and we let in more users. Once things settle down more, we’ll be able to offer more stable limits. Higher limits are available on custom and enterprise plans. Contact [REDACTED_EMAIL].\nL41: ##\nL42: \nL43: cite11†​ L44: \nL45: Aliases\nL46: \nL47: An alias is a model name that resolves to a versioned model ID. Send it in the `model` field like any other name.\nL48: \nL49: Alias  | Points to  | Meaning\nL50: --- | --- | ---\nL51: `jev-latest`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent stable, official release. The default in our client SDKs, and the name the examples in these docs use.\nL52: `jev-preview`  | `jev-1.13.0`  | The most recent release, whether or not it is an official one. Moves ahead of `jev-latest` when a preview build is available.\nL53: `jev-preview` currently points to the same model as `jev-latest`. There is no preview build available right now.\nL54: \nL55: An alias moves when a new release ships, so the answers behind it can change without a change on your side. The response’s `model` field reports the versioned ID that answered, so you can log which model produced each result. If you have tuned confidence thresholds against a specific version, pin that version’s ID instead of the alias and move to the new one on your own schedule.\nL56: ##\nL57: \nL58: cite12†​ L59: \nL60: Customizing Jev\nL61: \nL62: Jev is not fine-tuned or LoRA-adapted with customer data. It is trained with cite13†RLCD to return calibrated decisions, and the same weights serve every account. You shape its answers to your domain through the request rather than through per-account weights:\nL63:   * Put your proprietary content, records, and reference material in the `state` field. See cite10†State .\nL64:   * Encode your domain rules and boundary cases in the `instructions` and `criteria` of each question. See cite14†How to build with TypeSafe and cite15†Advanced: structure .\nL65:   * Decompose broad judgments into atomic questions and combine the outputs in code. See cite16†Composite scoring and the cite17†AutoResearch cookbook for training a downstream classical model on Jev’s probabilities.\nL66: ##\nL67: \nL68: cite18†​ L69: \nL70: Language support\nL71: \nL72: Jev accepts natural-language text. English is the primary training language and where accuracy is currently best. Other languages, including CJK scripts, are handled but not equally well; test on your own content before relying on Jev for a non-English workload, and pay close attention to cite19†Confidence when routing.\nL73: ##\nL74: \nL75: cite20†​ L76: \nL77: Data handling\nL78: \nL79: Jev is not trained on customer requests or responses. See cite21†Legal for the Data Processing Agreement, the Privacy Policy, and details on zero data retention (ZDR) for enterprise customers.\nL80: ##\nL81: \nL82: cite22†​ L83: \nL84: Listing models\nL85: \nL86: `GET /v1/models` returns the names your account can send in the `model` field, with a description and release date for each. It currently lists the aliases. Versioned IDs such as `jev-1.13.0` are accepted by the `model` field whether or not they appear in the list.\nL87: \nL88: cURL\nL89: \nL90: Python\nL91: JavaScript\nL92: \nL93:     `curl         "content": "Yes—but there’s a tighter limit that matters for our architecture.\n\nThe current official limits for **Jev 1.13**, which `jev-latest` points to, are:\n\n| Limit | What counts |\n|---|---|\n| **64k tokens per request** | Shared `state` + **all questions combined** |\n| **32k tokens** | Shared `state` + **the longest individual question** |\n\nJev reads the shared state once and evaluates the questions against it in parallel. [Source: TypeSafe model documentation](https://docs.typesafe.ai/models).\n\n**Our effective history limit is therefore slightly below 32k Jev tokens, not 64k.** Our state contains the complete history and target user message; we also need room for the question and message metadata. Having many messages can separately exhaust the 64k combined budget because we ask one question per message.\n\nSplitting the **questions** into multiple requests would help with the 64k limit, but **wouldn’t solve a history exceeding the 32k state limit**. That would require partitioning the history or using another curator with a larger context window—changing what information Jev can consider together.\n\nFor expanding the benchmark:\n\n- **Large context:** distinguish histories that fit the current architecture from those requiring a new strategy.\n- **Tool usage:** text tool outputs can be included in Jev’s state, but our runner needs support for tool-call/result relationships so omission doesn’t create invalid or misleading histories.\n\nWe can test those as separate extensions without changing the current best prompt."
